# Dassault Rafale

"Rafale" redirects here. For other uses, see [Rafale (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafale_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with Israeli defense technology company [Rafael Advanced Defense Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Advanced_Defense_Systems).

The **Dassault Rafale** (French: [\[ʁafal\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/French), literally meaning "gust of wind",[^cite_note-\:6-2] or "burst of fire" in a more military sense)[^cite_note-3] is a French [twin-engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twinjet), [canard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canard_\(aeronautics\)) [delta wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_wing), [multirole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multirole_combat_aircraft) [fighter aircraft](10929.md) designed and built by [Dassault Aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Aviation). Equipped with a wide range of weapons, the Rafale is intended to perform [air supremacy](537478.md), [interdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_interdiction), [aerial reconnaissance](20338498.md), [ground support](600792.md), in-depth strike, [anti-ship strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-ship_warfare) and [nuclear deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare) missions. Dassault markets the aircraft as an "omnirole" fighter due to its ability to perform multiple distinct mission roles during a single sortie.

In the late 1970s, the [French Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Air_Force) and [French Navy](383256.md) sought to replace and consolidate their existing fleets of aircraft. In order to reduce development costs and boost prospective sales, France entered into an arrangement with the UK, Germany, Italy and Spain to produce an agile multi-purpose "Future European Fighter Aircraft" (which would become the [Eurofighter Typhoon](167667.md)). Subsequent disagreements over workshare and differing requirements led France to pursue its own development programme. Dassault built a [technology demonstrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prototype) that first flew in July 1986 as part of an eight-year flight-test programme, paving the way for approval of the project.

The Rafale is distinct from other European fighters of its era in that it is almost entirely built by one country, France, involving most of France's major [defence contractors](608296.md), such as [Dassault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Aviation), [Thales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thales_Group) and [Safran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safran). Many of the aircraft's avionics and features, such as [direct voice input](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_voice_input), the [RBE2 AA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RBE2#Further_development) [active electronically scanned array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_electronically_scanned_array) (AESA) radar and the *[optronique secteur frontal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optronique_secteur_frontal)* [infra-red search and track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infra-red_search_and_track) (IRST) sensor, were domestically developed and produced for the Rafale programme. Originally scheduled to enter service in 1996, the Rafale suffered significant delays due to post-[Cold War](325329.md) budget cuts and changes in priorities. There are three main variants: Rafale C single-seat land-based version, Rafale B twin-seat land-based version, and Rafale M single-seat carrier-based version.

Introduced in 2001, the Rafale is being produced for both the French Air Force and for [carrier-based](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrier-based_aircraft) operations in the French Navy.[^cite_note-Operational_Rafale_HighBeam-1] It has been marketed for export to several countries, and was selected for purchase by the [Egyptian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force), the [Indian Air Force](403355.md), the [Indian Navy](604522.md), the [Qatar Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar_Air_Force), the [Hellenic Air Force](363254.md), the [Croatian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_Air_Force), the [Indonesian Air Force](8842734.md), the [United Arab Emirates Air Force](10815770.md) and the [Serbian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Air_Force). The Rafale is considered one of the most advanced and capable warplanes in the world,[^cite_note-4] and among the most successful internationally.[^cite_note-\:7-5] It has been used in combat over Afghanistan, Libya, Mali, Iraq, Syria, and by India near its border with Pakistan.[^cite_note-6]

## Development

### Background

In the mid-1970s, the [French Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Air_Force) (*Armée de l'Air*) and [French Navy](383256.md) (*Marine Nationale*) had separate requirements for a new generation of fighters to replace those in or about to enter service.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200292-7] Because their requirements were similar, and to reduce cost, both services issued a common request for proposal.[^cite_note-Rafale_backgd-8] In 1975, the country's Ministry of Aviation initiated studies for a new aircraft to complement the upcoming and smaller [Dassault Mirage 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_2000), with each aircraft optimized for differing roles.[^cite_note-\:8-9]

The Rafale aircraft development programme was the end product of efforts by various European countries for a common fighter aircraft. In 1979, Dassault-Breguet (later Dassault Aviation) joined the [MBB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messerschmitt-B%C3%B6lkow-Blohm)/[BAe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BAe) "European Collaborative Fighter" project which was renamed the "European Combat Aircraft" (ECA).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButtler2000134-10] The company contributed the aerodynamic layout of a prospective twin-engine, single-seat fighter; however, the project collapsed in 1981 due to differing operational requirements of each partner country.[^cite_note-Fiszer_p41-11] In 1983, the "Future European Fighter Aircraft" (FEFA) programme was initiated, bringing together France, Italy, Spain, West Germany and the United Kingdom to jointly develop a new fighter, although the latter three had their own aircraft developments.[^cite_note-Eden_p168-12] French officials envisioned a lightweight, multirole aircraft that—in addition to fulfilling both air force and [naval roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrier-based_aircraft)—it was believed, would be attractive on the export fighter market. This was in contrast to the British requirement for a heavy long-range interceptor. France also demanded a lead role, with the commensurate technical and industrial primacy, whereas the other countries were accepting of a more egalitarian programme structure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorellHoffman198912-13][^cite_note-15]

There was little common ground between France and the other members of this project, but by 1983, the five countries had agreed on a European Staff Target for a future fighter. Nevertheless, differences persisted, and so France withdrew from the multilateral talks in July 1985 to preserve the technological independence of its fighter aircraft industry. West Germany, the UK and Italy opted out and established a new European Fighter Aircraft (EFA) programme.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200292-7] In [Turin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turin), on 2 August 1985, West Germany, the UK and Italy agreed to go ahead with the EFA, and confirmed that France, along with Spain, had chosen not to proceed as a member of the project.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17] Despite pressure from France, Spain rejoined the EFA project in early September 1985. The four-nation project eventually resulted in the [Eurofighter Typhoon](167667.md)'s development.[^cite_note-18]



Rafale logo

In France, the government proceeded with its own programme. The [Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(France\)) required an aircraft capable of air-to-air and air-to-ground, [all-day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_fighter) and adverse weather operations. As France was the sole developer of the Rafale's [airframe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airframe), [avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avionics), [propulsion system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_engine) and [armament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_aircraft_weapons), the resultant aircraft was to replace a multitude of aircraft in the [French Armed Forces](10724.md). The Rafale would perform roles previously filled by an assortment of specialised platforms, including the [Jaguar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPECAT_Jaguar), [Mirage F1C/CR/CT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_F1), Mirage 2000C/-5/[N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_2000N/2000D) in the French Air Force, and the [F-8P Crusader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vought_F-8_Crusader), [Étendard IVP/M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_%C3%89tendard_IV) and [Super Étendard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault-Breguet_Super_%C3%89tendard) in French Naval Aviation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200292-7][^cite_note-19]

### Demonstration

At the same time as the multinational talks were occurring, Dassault-Breguet had been busy designing its *Avion de Combat Experimental* (ACX). During late 1978, prior to France's joining of the ECA, Dassault received contracts for the development of project ACT 92 (*Avion de Combat Tactique*, meaning "Tactical Combat Airplane"). The following year, the National Office for Aviation Studies and Research began studying the possible configurations of the new fighter under the codename *Rapace* ("Bird of Prey"). By March 1980, the number of configurations had been narrowed down to four, two of which had a combination of [canards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canard_\(aeronautics\)), [delta wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_wing) and a single [vertical tail-fin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertical_stabilizer).[^cite_note-Fiszer_p41-11] The ACX project was given political impetus when the French government awarded a contract for two (later reduced to one) technology demonstrator aircraft on 13 April 1983. The government and industry would each provide half of the development cost, with first flight to take place in 1986.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorellHoffman198914-20] At the time, there was no guarantee that the effort would result in a full-scale development programme, and the aircraft remained a purely "proof-of concept" test vehicle. In an effort to harmonize design specifics with the requirements of other countries while collaboration talks were being held, Dassault sized the ACX aircraft in the 9.5 tonne range. After France decided to pull out of the multilateral talks, designers focused on a more compact size, as specified by the Air Force.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELenorovitz198679-21] The ACX programme was renamed *Rafale* ("squall") in April 1985.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEden2004169-22]



The Dassault Rafale A technology demonstrator

Construction of the Rafale A (ACX) technology demonstrator started in 1984. It had a length of 15.8 m (52 ft), a wingspan of 11 m (36 ft), and a 9.5 t (21,000 lb) empty weight. The austere aircraft lacked in major subsystems, and had the minimal cockpit systems and a [fly-by-wire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly-by-wire) [flight control system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_flight_control_system) for the validation of the design's basic airframe-engine layout. The company desired to use the Rafale A to continue the company approach of risk reduction through incremental improvement and to test the aerodynamically unstable delta wing-canard configuration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorellHoffman198918-23]The aircraft was Dassault's 92nd prototype in 40 years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELorellHoffman198950-24] At the time of its construction, the aircraft had two 68.8 kN (15,500 lbf) [General Electric F404](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_F404) engines that were then in service with the F/A-18 Hornet, pending the availability of the [Snecma M88](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snecma_M88) [turbofan engines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbofan_engine).[^cite_note-Fiszer_p42-25][^cite_note-Spick_2000_p433-26] It was rolled out in December 1985 at [Saint-Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Cloud), and on 4 July 1986, made its first flight from the company's Istres test facility in southern France, piloted by Guy Mitaux-Maurouard. During the one-hour flight, the aircraft reached an altitude of 11,000 m (36,000 ft) and a speed of Mach 1.3.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedbon1986b4-27] The aircraft participated in the Farnborough air show the following month.

The aircraft participated in an intensive [flight test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_test) programme that saw it simulate air force and naval operations. The test vehicle flew approaches to the carrier [*Clemenceau*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_aircraft_carrier_Clemenceau), and also tested for coordination with [*Foch*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_aircraft_carrier_Foch). By 1987, the aircraft had been flown by Air Force, Navy and CEV test pilots. Its port-side F404 engine was replaced with the 72.9 kN (16,400 lbf) M88 in early 1990,[^cite_note-Fiszer_p42-25] and the aircraft flew under the updated powerplant configuration in May 1990.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200292-7][^cite_note-FI_1991_p34-28] The aircraft thereafter attained a speed of Mach 1.4 without the use of [engine reheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afterburner), thereby demonstrating supercruise.[^cite_note-29] The Rafale A was used until January 1994, and was retired after 867 sorties.[^cite_note-30]

The early successful demonstration programme increased French industry and government confidence in the viability of a full-scale development programme for the Rafale. In June 1987, French prime minister [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac) declared that the government would proceed with the project. A contract for four pre-production aircraft (one Rafale C, two Rafale Ms and one Rafale B) was awarded on 21 April 1988 for a test and validation programme.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-FI_1991_p34-28] There was nevertheless government uncertainty in the programme, as it was expected to cost some Ffr120 billion (1988 francs) in total development and procurement costs.[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33] Prime minister Michel Rocard was concerned about the state of the project and the failure of the previous government to secure cooperation with other countries, but stated that, "It is inconceivable that we should not be able to build the weapons necessary for our independence".[^cite_note-34] France had earlier entered unsuccessful talks with Belgium, Denmark, the Netherlands, and Norway, about the possible collaboration on the project.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESedbon1986a11-35]

### Testing



The Rafale C preproduction aircraft

To meet the various roles expected of the new aircraft, the Air Force required two variants: the single-seat Rafale C (*chasseur*, meaning "fighter") and the Rafale B (*biplace*, "two-seater"). Its first flight on 19 May 1991 occurred at the company's test facility in Istres. This signalled the start of a test programme which primarily aimed to test the M88-2 engines, [human-machine interface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/User_interface) and weapons, and expand the [flight envelope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_envelope).[^cite_note-Eden_p169-36] Due to budgetary constraints, the second single-seat prototype was never built.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200292–93-37] The aircraft differed significantly from the Rafale A demonstrator. Although superficially similar to the heavier test vehicle, the aircraft was smaller, with a length of 15.3 m (50 ft) and a wingspan of 10.9 m (36 ft).[^cite_note-AW\&ST_1990_p21-38]It was less detectable by radar due to the canopy being gold-plated and the addition of radar-absorbent materials; Dassault had also removed the dedicated airbrake.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200293-39] The sole Rafale B two-seat preproduction aircraft, B01, made its first flight on 30 April 1993, and served as a platform for testing of weapons and fire-control systems, including the RBE2 radar and the SPECTRA electronic warfare suite.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200293-39]

The first of two Rafale M (*maritime*, "naval") prototypes, M01, made its maiden flight on 12 December 1991, followed by the second on 8 November 1993.[^cite_note-Eden_p169-36] These aircraft differed from the air force variants in having reinforced structure to allow the aircraft to operate aboard ships, and provision for a tail hook and an in-built ladder, which increased the weight of the Rafale M by 500 kg (1,100 lb) over other production variants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200294–95-40] Since France has no land-based catapult test facility, catapult trials were carried out in mid-1992 and early 1993 at the [United States Navy](20518076.md) facility at [NAS Lakehurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakehurst_Maxfield_Field), [New Jersey](21648.md). The aircraft then carried out shipboard trials aboard *Foch* in April 1993.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200295-41] The aircraft conducted landings and launches from the nuclear-powered aircraft carrier *[Charles de Gaulle](639127.md)* in July 1999. Testing showed that the aircraft had the ability to land with significant loads of unexpended ordnance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200295-41]

### Production

The Rafale B was initially expected to be just a trainer, but the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) showed that a second crew member was invaluable on strike and [reconnaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconnaissance_aircraft) missions. The Air Force therefore switched its preferences towards the two-seater, and planned that the variant would constitute 60 percent of the Rafale fleet.[^cite_note-Eden_p170-42] The service originally planned to order 250 Rafales, later reduced to 234 aircraft, 95 "C" and 139 "B" models"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2002108-43] and then to 212 aircraft.[^cite_note-Eden_p170-42] The Navy originally planned to order 86 Rafales, which was reduced to 60 due to budget cuts,[^cite_note-Gaines_p80-44][^cite_note-Eden_p170-42] 25 M single-seaters and 35 two-seat Ns.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams2002108-43] The two-seater was later cancelled.

The ACX and subsequent production Rafale was designed in a "virtual" format. Dassault used the experience and technical expertise of its sister company [Dassault Systèmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Syst%C3%A8mes), which had invented the [CATIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CATIA) (Computer Aided Three-dimensional Interactive Application) system, a three-dimensional [computer-aided design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer-aided_design) and [computer-aided manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer-aided_manufacturing) (CAD/CAM) [software suite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software_suite) that became standard across the industry.[^cite_note-Cook_p22-45] CATIA enabled digitization and efficiency improvements throughout the programme, as it implemented recently developed processes such as [digital mockup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_mockup) and [product data management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Product_data_management) (PDM). Engineers worked directly with computers in generating 3D models of the aircraft, and took advantage of the design software in facilitating machine-tool preparation. The system consisted of 15GB databases of each of the Rafale's components, assisting with various aspects of the design, manufacture and through-life support.[^cite_note-Cook_p22-45] The computer-aided arrangement also simplified routine maintenance.

Production of the first aircraft series formally started in December 1992, but was suspended in November 1995 due to political and economic uncertainty, and resumed in January 1997 after the Ministry of Defence and Dassault agreed on a 48-aircraft (28 firm and 20 options) production run with delivery between 2002 and 2007.[^cite_note-Eden_p170-42] A further order of 59 Rafale F3s was announced in December 2004.[^cite_note-46] In November 2009 the French government ordered an additional 60 aircraft to take the total order for the French Air Force and Navy to 180.[^cite_note-Order180-47]



A French Navy Rafale M landing aboard [USS *George H.W. Bush*](156573.md) - 11 May 2018

The Rafale is manufactured almost entirely in France, except for some imported non-sensitive components.[^cite_note-Sites_and_production_shares-48] Different components are produced in various plants across the country, including the fuselage in Paris, wings in Martignas, and fins in Biarritz, with final assembly taking place in Merignac near Bordeaux.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESvitakBruneau201560-49] Dassault carries out 60% of the work, its partner [Thales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thales_Group) 25%, and its other partner [Safran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safran) 15%.[^cite_note-50] The three companies rely on a network of 500 subcontractors, many of which are [small and medium enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_and_medium_enterprises), providing work for 7,000 direct and indirect employees.[^cite_note-Sites_and_production_shares-48] As of 2012, each fighter took 24 months to manufacture, with an annual production rate of eleven aircraft.[^cite_note-Sites_and_production_shares-48]

The Rafale was originally planned to enter service in 1995. The aircraft's development proceeded on time, on budget, and without major difficulties. However, the project needed to compete with other defense acquisition programmes for a dwindling national defense budget.[^cite_note-AW\&ST_1990_p21-38] This occurred in a political environment in which the chief security threat, the Soviet Union, no longer existed. The French government consequently reduced Rafale orders, which Dassault and other companies involved claimed impeded production management and led to higher costs, and delayed the entry of the aircraft into service.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlight_International199427-51][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESparaco199629-52] At one stage, French naval authorities investigated the possibility of acquiring used F/A-18s to replace the obsolete F-8 for its carriers,[^cite_note-53] but the French government intended an all-Rafale fleet, and did not go ahead with the plan. Deliveries of the Rafale M were subsequently given a high priority to replace the Navy's aged F-8 fighters. In the words of a naval official, "Although we lost the battle for the F/A-18s, I guess you could say that we had at least some success by 'persuading' the government to give us initial delivery priority".[^cite_note-AW\&ST_1990_p21-38] The first production Rafale B took its first flight on 24 November 1998,[^cite_note-Fiszer_p44-54] followed by the first Rafale M for the French Navy on 7 July 1999.[^cite_note-55]

In March 2025, Dassault Aviation announced an increase in production in anticipation of more orders from France and other countries. The company's chief executive, Eric Trappier, said they plan to deliver three aircraft per month in 2025, increasing to four per month from 2028 to 2029.[^cite_note-56]

On 5 June 2025, Dassault Aviation announced its partnership with India's [Tata Advanced Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tata_Advanced_Systems) (TASL) for the production of Rafale's [fuselage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuselage) in India after signing four Production Transfer Agreements. The manufacturing will be taken up in TASL's [Hyderabad](37534.md) facility. The first fuselage will roll out from [fiscal year](5752099.md) of 2028 with a production rate of two units per month. This will be the first time the Rafale's fuselage is manufactured outside of France.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] Since earlier, five different parts of Rafale is being manufactured by [Dassault Reliance Aerospace Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Aviation#Production) (DRAL) in [Nagpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagpur), India.[^cite_note-60]

### Upgrades and replacement

The Rafale has been designed with an open software architecture that facilitates straightforward upgrades. Dassault and its industry partners have therefore undertaken continuous tests and development primarily aimed at progressively improving the aircraft's sensors and avionics, and to allow additional armament integration. In 2011, upgrades under consideration included a software radio and satellite link, a new laser-targeting pod, smaller bombs and enhancements to the aircraft's data-fusion capacity.[^cite_note-61] In July 2012, fleetwide upgrades of the Rafale's battlefield communications and interoperability capabilities commenced.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63]

At one stage, French officials were reportedly considering equipping the Rafale to launch [miniaturised satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miniaturized_satellite).[^cite_note-64]

In January 2014, the defence ministry announced that funds had been allocated towards the development of the F3R standard. The standard includes the integration of the Meteor BVR missile, among other weapons and software updates.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] The standard was validated in 2018.[^cite_note-67]

Development work started on the F4 standard in 2019.[^cite_note-F4_standard-68] The design received radar and sensor upgrades that facilitate the detection of airborne stealth targets at long range, as well as improved capabilities in the helmet-mounted display. With improved communications equipment, it is also more effective in network-centric warfare. Flight tests were conducted starting in 2021 and the first F4-standard aircraft was delivered in 2023.[^cite_note-F4_standard-68][^cite_note-69] Previous aircraft will be upgraded to the standard, with a further 30 aircraft to be ordered in 2023.[^cite_note-F4_standard-68]

The total programme cost, as of FY2013, was around €45.9 billion,[^cite_note-Francebudget2013-70] which translated to a unit programme cost of approximately €160.5 million. This figure takes in account improved hardware of the F3 standard, and which includes development costs over a period of 40 years, including inflation.[^cite_note-71] The unit [flyaway price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flyaway_cost) as of 2010 was €101.1 million for the F3+ version.[^cite_note-fighter_costs-72]

The F5 standard will have more powerful engines, carry the ASN4G hypersonic nuclear missile, RBE2 XG radar, improved survivability and data links, and will be accompanied by an unmanned combat air vehicle (UCAV) weighing more than ten metric tons (9.8 long tons; 11 short tons).[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]

The Rafale is planned to be the French Air and Space Force's primary combat aircraft until at least 2040.[^cite_note-OpAir-76] In 2018, Dassault announced the successor to the Rafale as the [New Generation Fighter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Generation_Fighter). This fighter aircraft, under development by [Dassault Aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Aviation) and [Airbus Defence and Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_Defence_and_Space), is to replace France's Rafale, Germany's Eurofighter Typhoon, and Spain's F/A-18 Hornet in the 2030–2040 timeframe.[^cite_note-77] In June 2026, it was announced that the NGF had been cancelled due to disputes between the two companies, leaving the Rafale's replacement aircraft uncertain with 14 years left in its role as the primary combat aircraft of the French Air and Space Force.

## Design

### Overview

The Rafale was developed as a modern jet fighter with a very high level of agility; Dassault chose to combine a delta wing with [active close-coupled canard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canard_\(aeronautics\)#Close_coupling) to maximize maneuverability. The aircraft is capable of withstanding from −3.6 *g* to 9 *g* (10.5 *g* on Rafale solo display and a maximum of 11*g* can be reached in case of emergency).[^cite_note-AIRFAN431-78][^cite_note-Defencenews-79] The Rafale is an [aerodynamically unstable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relaxed_stability) aircraft and uses [digital fly-by-wire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fly-by-wire#Digital_systems) flight controls to artificially enforce and maintain stability.[^cite_note-Defencenews-79][^cite_note-81] The aircraft's canards also act to reduce the minimum landing speed to 115 [knots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knot_\(unit\)) (213 km/h; 132 mph); while in flight, airspeeds as low as 15 knots (28 km/h; 17 mph) have been observed during training missions.[^cite_note-Defencenews-79] According to simulations by Dassault, the Rafale has sufficient low speed performance to operate from [STOBAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STOBAR)-configured aircraft carriers, and can take off using a ski-jump with no modifications.[^cite_note-82]

The Rafale M features a greatly reinforced [undercarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landing_gear) to cope with the additional stresses of naval landings, an [arrestor hook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tailhook), and "jump strut" nosewheel, which only extends during short takeoffs, including [catapult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_catapult) launches.[^cite_note-Eden_p169-36] It also features a built-in ladder, carrier-based [microwave landing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microwave_landing_system), and the new fin-tip Telemir system for syncing the [inertial navigation system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertial_navigation_system) to external equipment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200294-83] Altogether, the naval modifications of the Rafale M increase its weight by 500 kilograms (1,100 lb) compared to other variants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200295-41] The Rafale M retains about 95 percent [commonality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonality) with Air Force variants including,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForsberg1994213-84] although unusual for carrier-based aircraft, being unable to fold its multi-spar wings to reduce storage space. The size constraints were offset by the introduction of [*Charles de Gaulle*](639127.md), France's first [nuclear-powered carrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_marine_propulsion), which was considerably larger than previous carriers, *Foch* and *Clemenceau*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200294-83]

Although not a full-aspect [stealth aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stealth_aircraft), the cost of which was viewed as unacceptably excessive, the Rafale was designed for a reduced [radar cross-section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_cross-section) (RCS) and [infrared signature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared_signature).[^cite_note-richardson_114-5-85][^cite_note-Forsberg_215-86] In order to reduce the RCS, changes from the initial technology demonstrator include a reduction in the size of the tail-fin, fuselage reshaping, repositioning of the engine [air inlets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intake) underneath the aircraft's wing, and the extensive use of [composite materials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_material) and [serrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serration) patterns for the construction of the trailing edges of the wings and canards.[^cite_note-OpAir-76][^cite_note-richardson_114-5-85] Seventy percent of the Rafale's surface area is composite.[^cite_note-87] Many of the features designed to reduce the Rafale's visibility to threats remain classified.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80]

### Cockpit

The Rafale's [glass cockpit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass_cockpit) was designed around the principle of [data fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_fusion)—a central computer selects and prioritises information to display to pilots for simpler command and control.[^cite_note-88] For displaying information gathered from a range of sensors across the aircraft, the cockpit features a wide-angle holographic [head-up display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-up_display) (HUD) system, two head-down [flat-panel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_panel_display) colour [multi-function displays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-function_display) (MFDs) as well as a central [collimated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collimator_sight) display. These displays have been strategically placed to minimise pilot distraction from the external environment.<[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJarrett2005144–45-89] Some displays feature a [touch interface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touch_interface) for ease of [human–computer interaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human%E2%80%93computer_interaction) (HCI).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiszer200543-90] A [head-mounted display](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-mounted_display) (HMD) remains to be integrated to take full advantage of its MICA missiles.[^cite_note-Collins-91][^cite_note-92][^cite_note-DID_Rafale-93] The cockpit is fully compatible with [night vision goggles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_vision_device) (NVG).[^cite_note-Collins-91] The primary flight controls are arranged in a [hands-on-throttle-and-stick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HOTAS) (HOTAS)-compatible configuration, with a right-handed [side-stick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Side-stick) controller and a left-handed throttle.[^cite_note-Collins-91] The seat is inclined rearwards at an angle of 29° to improve [g-force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-force) tolerance during manoeuvring and to provide a less restricted external pilot view.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJarrett200556-94]



Forward section of Rafale on display at the [Paris Air Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Air_Show), 2005

Great emphasis has been placed on pilot workload minimisation across all operations.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80] Among the features of the highly digitised cockpit is an integrated [direct voice input](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_voice_input) (DVI) system, allowing a range of aircraft functions to be controlled by spoken voice commands, simplifying the pilot's access to many of the controls.[^cite_note-Collins-91] For safety reasons, generally DVI is deliberately not employed for safety-critical elements of the aircraft's operation, such as the final release of weapons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJarrett2005270–271-95]

In the area of [life support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_support_\(aviation\)), the Rafale is fitted with a [Martin-Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin-Baker) Mark 16F "zero-zero" [ejection seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejection_seat), capable of operation at zero speed and zero altitude. An on-board [oxygen generating system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen_generating_system), developed by [Air Liquide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Liquide), eliminates the need to carry bulky oxygen canisters.[^cite_note-96] The Rafale's flight computer has been programmed to counteract pilot disorientation and to employ automatic recovery of the aircraft during negative flight conditions. The [auto-pilot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto-pilot) and [autothrottle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autothrottle) controls are also integrated, and are activated by switches located on the primary flight controls.[^cite_note-Collins-91] An intelligent [flight suit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_suit) worn by the pilot is automatically controlled by the aircraft to counteract in response to calculated g-forces.[^cite_note-97]

### Avionics and equipment

The Rafale core avionics systems employ an [integrated modular avionics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_modular_avionics) (IMA), called MDPU (modular data processing unit). This architecture hosts all the main aircraft functions such as the [flight management system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_management_system), data fusion, [fire control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire-control_system), and the man-machine interface.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80][^cite_note-98] The total value of the radar, electronic communications and self-protection equipment is about 30 percent of the cost of the entire aircraft.[^cite_note-Paris_Air_Show-99] The IMA has since been installed upon several upgraded Mirage 2000 fighters,[^cite_note-100] and incorporated into the civilian airliner, the [Airbus A380](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A380).[^cite_note-101] According to Dassault, the IMA greatly assists combat operations via data fusion, the continuous integration and analysis of the various sensor systems throughout the aircraft, and has been designed for the incorporation of new systems and avionics throughout the Rafale's service life.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80]



Annotated diagram of [SPECTRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thales_SPECTRA)'s elements

The Rafale features an integrated [defensive-aids system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defensive_aids_system) named [SPECTRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thales_SPECTRA), which protects the aircraft against airborne and ground threats, developed as a joint venture between Thales and [MBDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MBDA).[^cite_note-102] Various methods of detection, [jamming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radar_jamming_and_deception), and [decoying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flare_\(countermeasure\)) have been incorporated, and the system has been designed to be highly reprogrammable for addressing new threats and incorporating additional sub-systems in the future.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] Operations over Libya were greatly assisted by SPECTRA, allowing Rafales to perform missions independently from the support of dedicated [Suppression of Enemy Air Defences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suppression_of_Enemy_Air_Defenses) (SEAD) platforms.[^cite_note-Dummies-105]

The Rafale's ground attack capability is heavily reliant upon sensory targeting pods,[^cite_note-106] such as [Thales Optronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thales_Optronics)'s Reco New Generation/Areos reconnaissance pod and [Damocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thales_Damocles) electro-optical/laser [designation pod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targeting_pods).[^cite_note-Paris_Air_Show-99] Together, these systems provide targeting information, enable tactical reconnaissance missions, and are integrated with the Rafale's IMA architecture to provide analysed data feeds to friendly units and ground stations, as well as to the pilot.[^cite_note-avitod_jul_2011-107] Damocles provides targeting information to the various armaments carried by the Rafale and is directly integrated with the Rafale's [VHF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VHF_Data_Link)/[UHF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra_high_frequency) secure radio to communicate target information with other aircraft. It also performs other key functions such as aerial optical surveillance and is integrated with the navigation system as a [FLIR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FLIR).[^cite_note-avitod_jul_2011-107]

The Damocles designation pod was described as "lacking competitiveness" when compared to rivals such as the Sniper and [LITENING](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LITENING) pods;[^cite_note-108] so work began on an upgraded pod, designated Damocles XF, with additional sensors and added ability to transmit live video feeds.[^cite_note-109] A new Thales targeting pod, the Talios, was officially unveiled at the 2014 Farnborough Air Show[^cite_note-110] and is expected to be integrated on the Rafale by 2018.[^cite_note-111] Thales' Areos reconnaissance pod is an all-weather, night-and-day-capable reconnaissance system employed on the Rafale, and provides a significantly improved reconnaissance capability over preceding platforms.[^cite_note-FoxThree14-112][^cite_note-113] Areos has been designed to perform reconnaissance under various mission profiles and condition, using multiple day/night sensors and its own independent communications datalinks.[^cite_note-avitod_jul_2011-107]

### Radar and sensors



The [OSF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optronique_secteur_frontal) is visible above the [nose cone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nose_cone), below the windscreen and to the side of the [refueling probe](237949.md)

The Rafale was first outfitted with the Thales [RBE2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RBE2) [passive electronically scanned multi-mode radar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passive_electronically_scanned_array). Thales claims to have achieved increased levels of situational awareness as compared to earlier aircraft through the earlier detection and tracking of multiple air targets for close combat and long-range interception, as well as real-time generation of three-dimensional maps for [terrain-following](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrain-following_radar) and the real-time generation of [high resolution ground maps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_aperture_radar) for navigation and targeting.[^cite_note-smart_discreet-114] In early 1994, it was reported that technical difficulties with the radar had delayed the Rafale's development by six months.[^cite_note-Forsberg_215-86] In September 2006, *[Flight International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_International)* reported the Rafale's unit cost had significantly increased due to additional development work to improve the RBE2's detection range.[^cite_note-115]

The [RBE2 AA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RBE2#Further_development) [active electronically scanned array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_electronically_scanned_array) (AESA) radar now replaces the previous passively scanned RBE2. The RBE2 AA is reported to deliver a greater detection range of 200 km,[^cite_note-AIRFAN432-116] improved reliability and reduced maintenance demands over the preceding radar.[^cite_note-fg_oct_2012-117] A Rafale demonstrator began test flights in 2002 and has totaled 100 flight hours as of December 2011. By December 2009, production of the pre-series RBE2 AA radars was underway.[^cite_note-FoxThree14-112] In early October 2012, the first Rafale equipped with an RBE2 AA radar arrived at Mont-de-Marsan Air Base for operational service (the development was described by Thales and Dassault as "on time and on budget").[^cite_note-fg_oct_2012-117] By early 2014, the first Air Force front-line squadron were supposed to receive Rafales equipped with the AESA radar, following the French Navy which was slated to receive AESA-equipped Rafales starting in 2013.[^cite_note-FoxThree16-118]

To enable the Rafale to perform in the air supremacy role, it includes several passive sensor systems. The front-sector electro-optical system or [Optronique Secteur Frontal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optronique_secteur_frontal) (OSF), developed by Thales, is completely integrated within the aircraft and can operate both in the visible and [infrared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infra-red_search_and_track) wavelengths.[^cite_note-119] The OSF enables the deployment of infrared missiles such as the [MICA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MICA_\(missile\)) at [beyond visual range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyond_visual_range) distances; it can also be used for detecting and identifying airborne targets, as well as those on the ground and at sea.[^cite_note-120] Dassault describes the OSF as being immune to jamming and capable of providing covert long-range surveillance.[^cite_note-smart_discreet-114] In 2012, an improved version of the OSF was deployed operationally.[^cite_note-fg_oct_2012-117]

### Armament and standards

Initial deliveries of the Rafale M were to the *F1* ("France 1") standard, which were equipped for the air-to-air interceptor combat duties, but lacked any armament for air-to-ground operations. The F1 standard became operational in 2004.[^cite_note-Squadrons-121][^cite_note-Wertheim-122] Later deliveries were to the "F2" standard, which added the capability for conducting air-to-ground operations; the first F2 standard Rafale M was delivered to the French Navy in May 2006.[^cite_note-123] Starting in 2008 onwards, Rafale deliveries have been to the nuclear-capable F3 standard that also added reconnaissance with the Areos reconnaissance pod,[^cite_note-Squadrons-121] and it has been reported that all aircraft built to the earlier F1 and F2 standards are to be upgraded to become F3s.[^cite_note-Collins-91][^cite_note-DID_Rafale-93]



The Rafale's weapons

F3 standard Rafales are capable of undertaking many different mission roles with a range of equipment, namely air defence/superiority missions with Mica IR and EM air-to-air missiles, and precision ground attacks typically using [SCALP EG](306908.md) cruise missiles and [AASM Hammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AASM_Hammer) air-to-surface missiles. In addition, anti-shipping missions could be carried out using the AM39 [Exocet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exocet) [sea skimming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_skimming) missile, while reconnaissance flights would use a combination of onboard and external pod-based sensor equipment. Furthermore, the aircraft could conduct nuclear strikes when armed with [ASMP-A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air-Sol_Moyenne_Port%C3%A9e) missiles.[^cite_note-124] In 2010, France ordered 200 [MBDA Meteor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteor_\(missile\)) [beyond-visual-range air-to-air missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyond-visual-range_missile) (BVRAAM) which greatly increases the distance at which the Rafale can engage aerial targets.[^cite_note-fox_three-125][^cite_note-126]

The F4 standard program was launched on 20 March 2017 by the French ministry of defence.[^cite_note-127] The first F4.1 standard test aircraft was delivered in March 2023.[^cite_note-128]

For compatibility with armaments of varying types and origins, the Rafale's onboard store management system is compliant with [MIL-STD-1760](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIL-STD-1760), an electrical interface between an aircraft and its carriage stores, thereby simplifying the incorporation of many of their existing weapons and equipment.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80] The Rafale is typically outfitted with 14 [hardpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardpoint) (only 13 on Rafale M version), five of which are suitable for heavy armament or equipment such as auxiliary fuel tanks, and has a maximum external load capacity of nine tons. In addition to the above equipment, the Rafale carries the 30 mm [GIAT 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GIAT_30) revolver cannon and can be outfitted with a range of laser-guided bombs and ground-attack munitions.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80] According to Dassault, the Rafale's onboard mission systems enable ground attack and air-to-air combat operations to be carried out within a single sortie, with many functions capable of simultaneous execution in conjunction with another, increasing survivability and versatility.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80]

### Engines

Main article: [Snecma M88](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snecma_M88)



Closeup of the rear of the airframe and the two engine nozzles

The Rafale is fitted with two Snecma M88 engines, each capable of providing up to 50 kilonewtons (11,000 pounds-force) of [dry thrust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_thrust?action=edit\&redlink=1) and 75 kN (17,000 lb<sub>f</sub>) with afterburners. The engines feature several advances, including a non-polluting combustion chamber, single-crystal turbine blades, [powder metallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Powder_metallurgy) disks, and technology to reduce radar and infrared signatures.[^cite_note-rafale_june_2005-80] The M88 enables the Rafale to supercruise while carrying four missiles and one drop tank.[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-M88AIAA-130]

Qualification of the M88-2 engine ended in 1996 and the first production engine was delivered by the end of the year.[^cite_note-M88_1999-131] Due to delays in engine production, the Rafale A demonstrator was initially powered by the General Electric F404 engine.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliams200292-7][^cite_note-132] In May 2010, a Rafale flew for the first time with the M88-4E engine, an upgraded variant with lower maintenance requirements than the preceding M88-2.[^cite_note-133] The engine is of a [modular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modularity) design for ease of construction and maintenance and to enable older engines to be retrofitted with improved subsections upon availability, such as existing M88-2s being upgraded to M88-4E standard.[^cite_note-M88_1999-131] There has been interest in more powerful M88 engines by potential export customers, such as the United Arab Emirates (UAE).[^cite_note-134] As of 2007, a [thrust vectoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_vectoring) variant of the engine designated as M88-3D was also under development.[^cite_note-Wertheim-122]

## Operational history

### France

#### French Naval Aviation



Rafale Ms aboard [USS *Theodore Roosevelt* (CVN-71)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Theodore_Roosevelt_\(CVN-71\)) in 2008

In December 2000, the [French Naval Aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Naval_Aviation) (*Aéronavale*), the air arm of the French Navy, received its first two Rafale M fighters. On 18 May the following year, the squadron *[Flottille 12F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Naval_Aviation#Units)*, which had previously operated the [F-8 Crusader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-8_Crusader), became the first squadron to operate the Rafale after it was officially re-activated prior to the delivery of the sixth Rafale.[^cite_note-Operational_Rafale_HighBeam-1] *Flottille 12F* immediately participated in *Trident d'Or* aboard the [aircraft carrier *Charles de Gaulle*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aircraft_carrier_Charles_de_Gaulle) with warships from ten other nations. During the [maritime exercise](1225714.md), the Navy tested the Rafale's avionics during simulated interceptions with various foreign aircraft, in addition to carrier take-offs and landings.[^cite_note-Operational_Rafale_HighBeam-1][^cite_note-Early_operations-135] After almost four years of training, the Rafale M was declared operational with the French Navy in June 2004.[^cite_note-Rafale_early_ops-136]

The Rafale M is fully compatible with [United States Navy](20518076.md) aircraft carriers and some French Navy pilots have qualified to fly the aircraft from US Navy flight decks.[^cite_note-137] On 4 June 2010, during an exercise on [USS *Harry S. Truman*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Harry_S._Truman), a French Rafale became the first jet fighter of a foreign navy to have its engine replaced on board an American aircraft carrier.[^cite_note-138]

In 2002, the Rafales were first deployed to a combat zone; seven Rafale Ms embarked aboard *Charles de Gaulle* of the French Navy during "[Mission Héraclès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_H%C3%A9racl%C3%A8s)", the French participation in "[Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom)". They flew from the aircraft carrier over Afghanistan, but the F1 standard precluded air-to-ground missions and the Rafale did not see any action. In March 2002, the aircraft carrier was stationed in the [Gulf of Oman](12896.md), where its complement of Rafales undertook training operations.[^cite_note-MacKenzie_p36-139] In June 2002, while *Charles de Gaulle* was in the Arabian Sea, Rafales conducted several patrols near the India-Pakistan border.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiszer200550-140][^cite_note-JED_report-141]

In 2016, Rafales operating from *Charles de Gaulle* struck targets associated with the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) (IS).[^cite_note-142]

In December 2015, American and French military officials reportedly discussed the possibility of French naval Rafale Ms flying combat missions from a US Navy [*Nimitz*-class](181160.md) aircraft carrier as soon as January 2017. This would enable continued French Navy operations against ISIL while *Charles de Gaulle* undergoes its year-and-a-half-long major refit, scheduled to begin in early 2017. Although Rafales have launched and landed on U.S. carriers to demonstrate interoperability, it would be the first time they would fly combat missions from one. As many as 18 Rafale Ms could be deployed on a carrier, although some room would have to be made for French Navy support crews familiar with maintaining the Rafale, as well as for spare parts and munitions. Operation Chesapeake, a test of this interoperability, was conducted in May 2018, when 12 Rafales of Flottilles 11F, 12F, and [17F](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flottille_17F), along with nearly 350 support personnel embarked aboard [USS *George H.W. Bush*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_George_H.W._Bush) for two weeks of carrier qualifications and exercises after conducting a month of shore based training at [Naval Air Station Oceana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Oceana).[^cite_note-143]

On 9 January 2025, [Rafale M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafale_M) conducted joint anti-aircraft drills with [Su-30MKI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-30MKI) and [Jaguar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEPECAT_Jaguar) aircraft of the [Indian Air Force](403355.md). The French Carrier Strike Group (CSG) centered on the *Charles de Gaulle*, the carrier air wing including Rafale M, her escort ships and fleet support ship *Jacques Chevallier* were on a visit to India between 4 and 9 January 2025 during the Mission [Clemenceau 25](639127.md#Clemenceau_25). Simultaneously, [INS *Mormugao*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INS_Mormugao) conducted joint navigational drills and [Maritime Partnership Exercise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Partnership_Exercise) with the escort ships.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145]

On June 8, 2026, a Rafale engaged a drone that had entered Latvian airspace.[^cite_note-Sytas-146]

#### French Air and Space Force



Formation of five Rafales making a flypast in 2006

In April 2005, the Air Force received its first three F2 standard Rafale Bs at the *[Centre d'expertise aérienne militaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_d'expertise_a%C3%A9rienne_militaire)* (CEAM, i.e. the Military Air Expertise Centre) at [Mont-de-Marsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mont-de-Marsan), where they were tasked to undertake operational evaluation and pilot conversion training.[^cite_note-Fiszer_p44-54] By this time, it was expected that [*Escadron de Chasse* (Fighter Squadron) *1/7*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/01.007_Fighter_Squadron_%22Provence%22) at Saint-Dizier would receive a nucleus of 8–10 Rafale F2s during the summer of 2006, in preparation for full operational service (with robust air-to-air and stand off air-to-ground precision attack capabilities) starting from mid-2007 (when *EC 1/7* would have about 20 aircraft, 15 two-seaters and five single-seaters).[^cite_note-Rafale_early_ops-136][^cite_note-147]

In 2007, a "crash program" upgrade on six Rafales enabled the use of laser-guided bombs in readiness for action in Afghanistan. Three of these aircraft of the Air Force were deployed to [Dushanbe](56658.md) in Tajikistan, while the three others were *Rafale Marine* of the Navy on board *Charles De Gaulle*.[^cite_note-Figaro-148] The first mission occurred on 12 March 2007, and the first [GBU-12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-12) was launched on 28 March in support of embattled Dutch troops in Southern Afghanistan, marking the operational début of the Rafale.[^cite_note-149] Between January 2009 and December 2011, a minimum of three Rafales were stationed at [Kandahar International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_International_Airport) to conduct operations in support of NATO ground forces.[^cite_note-150]

On 19 March 2011, French Rafales began conducting reconnaissance and [strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_\(attack\)) missions over Libya in [Opération Harmattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ration_Harmattan), in support of [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1973); initial targets were artillery pieces laying siege around the rebel city of [Benghazi](204114.md).[^cite_note-151] The Rafale could operate in Libya without the support of SEAD aircraft, using the onboard SPECTRA self-defence system instead.[^cite_note-Dummies-105] On 24 March 2011, it was reported that a Rafale had destroyed a [Libyan Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Air_Force_\(1951%E2%80%932011\)) [G-2/Galeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soko_G-2_Galeb) light attack/trainer aircraft on the runway.[^cite_note-152] During the deployment, Rafale destroyed multiple SAM systems of Libyan military using its geolocation feature and with a mix of different ammunition. Unlike other allied aircraft, the Rafale did not require any dedicated EW/EA aircraft for escort.[^cite_note-153]

Rafales typically conducted six-hour sorties over Libyan airspace, armed with four MICA air-to-air missiles, four or six AASM "Hammer" bombs, a Thales Damoclès targeting pod and two drop tanks.[^cite_note-Dummies-105] Each sortie needed multiple aerial refuelling operations from coalition tanker aircraft.[^cite_note-flightglobal-154] The AASM precision-guidance weapon system allowed the Rafale to conduct high-altitude bombing missions using bombs weighing between 125 and 1,000 kg (280 and 2,200 lb).[^cite_note-flightglobal-154] Reportedly, Rafale crews preferred to use GPS-guided munitions with greater reliability and range. SCALP weapons were deployed on only one or two sorties, such as against a Libyan [airbase at Al-Jufra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbase_at_Al-Jufra).[^cite_note-155] In 2011, aviation journalist Craig Hoyle speculated that the Rafale's Libyan performance is likely to impact export sales, noting that the Rafale had maintained a high operational rate throughout. Hoyle also noted that the conflict had led to several urgent operational requirements, including a lighter ground-attack munition and AASM modifications for [close air support](600792.md).[^cite_note-flightglobal-154]



A French Air Force Rafale B during [Operation Serval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Serval) in Mali, 2013

In January 2013, the Rafale took part in [Opération Serval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ration_Serval), the French military intervention in [support to the government of Mali](36856978.md) against the [Movement for Oneness and Jihad in West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_for_Oneness_and_Jihad_in_West_Africa).[^cite_note-156] The first mission was carried out on 13 January, when four Rafales took off from an airbase in France to strike rebel training camps, depots and facilities in the city of [Gao](1255721.md) in eastern Mali.[^cite_note-157] Subsequent airstrikes in the following days by Rafale and Mirage fighters were reportedly instrumental in the withdrawal of Islamist militant forces from [Timbuktu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timbuktu) and [Douentza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douentza).[^cite_note-158] Both Rafale and [Mirage 2000D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirage_2000D) aircraft used in the conflict have been based outside of North Africa, making use of aerial refuelling tanker aircraft to fly long range sorties across Algerian airspace and into Mali.[^cite_note-159]

In August 2013, it was proposed that France may halve the number of Rafales to be delivered over the next six years for a total of 26 aircraft to be delivered during this period; foreign export procurement have been viewed as critical to maintain production under this proposal. While production would be slowed, France would still receive the same number of Rafales overall.[^cite_note-160]

In September 2014, Rafales started reconnaissance missions over Iraq for [Opération Chammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op%C3%A9ration_Chammal), France's contribution to the international effort to combat IS militants. Six Rafales were initially tasked with identifying IS positions in support of [US airstrikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_military_intervention_against_ISIS), flying from [Al Dhafra Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Dhafra_Air_Base), UAE.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] On 18 September, Rafales joined American attack operations, launching four strikes near the Northern Iraqi town of [Zummar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zummar) that destroyed a logistics depot and killed dozens of IS fighters.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] In April 2018, during the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md), five Rafale Bs from the [Escadron de Chasse 1/4 Gascogne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escadron_de_Chasse_1/4_Gascogne) participated in the [2018 missile strikes against Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_missile_strikes_against_Syria). Each was loaded with two [SCALP EG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SCALP_EG) missiles.[^cite_note-165] French Air and Space Force Rafales were deployed to help blunt the [Iranian attack against Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2024_Iranian_strikes_against_Israel) on 13 April 2024 by shooting down an unspecified number of unmanned aerial vehicles. The Rafales, based in Jordan, were operating in Iraqi and Syrian airspace as part of Opération Chammal.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

During the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md), a French base in the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) suffered a drone strike in the early days of the war when France was otherwise uninvolved, prompting the French Government to deploy Rafales and other assets to the Middle East as part of a package meant to defend French interests in the region.[^cite_note-168] These planes have taken part in interceptions of Iranian Drones, with the first interceptions reported on the 4th of March and are primarily using MICA missiles.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-\:5-170] It was reported as of 20 March, that French Rafales brought down at least 60 Iranian drones over the UAE.[^cite_note-\:5-170]

### Egypt

In November 2014, Egypt was reportedly in negotiations with France to purchase 24 to 36 Rafales, subject to a financing and weapons package agreement.[^cite_note-171] By February 2015, the two countries were negotiating a loan from France's export credit agency to reach an export agreement for up to 24 Rafales. The condition for Egypt to buy the 12 additional fighters was to get [SCALP-EG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SCALP-EG) missiles, this was compromised by the [US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Traffic_in_Arms_Regulations) blocking the deal.[^cite_note-172] Egypt aimed for the deal's quick completion as to have them on display at the inauguration of the [Suez Canal expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Suez_Canal) in August 2015.[^cite_note-173]

On 16 February 2015, Egypt became the Rafale's first international customer when it officially ordered 24 Rafales,[^cite_note-174] as part of a larger deal, including a [FREMM multipurpose frigate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FREMM_multipurpose_frigate) and missiles, worth US\$5.9 billion (€5.2 billion).[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-F24-176] The order comprised 8 single-seat models and 16 two-seaters. In July 2015, a ceremony marking Egypt's acceptance of its first three Rafales, was held at Dassault's flight test center in [Istres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istres).[^cite_note-dassault-aviation1-177] In January 2016, Egypt received three more Rafales.[^cite_note-defenseworld.net-178] All six aircraft are two-seat models (Rafale DM) diverted from French Air Force deliveries.[^cite_note-179] Egypt received the third batch of three Rafales flown by Egyptian pilots from France in April 2017; this included the first single-seat model (Rafale EM) to be delivered to the [Egyptian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force).[^cite_note-auto-180] Egypt took delivery of the fourth batch of two Rafale EMs in July 2017.[^cite_note-181] The fifth batch, comprising the last 3 Rafale EMs, was delivered in November 2017, increasing the number in service to 14 Rafales.[^cite_note-defenceweb.co.za-182]

In June 2016, Egypt begun negotiations with Dassault to acquire 12 additional Rafales, intending to exercise an option of the first contract.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] An Egyptian delegation visited France in November 2017 for negotiations.[^cite_note-185] In May 2021, Egypt ordered 30 more Rafales in a contract worth \$4.5bn after France achieved making the SCALP EG missile [ITAR-free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITAR) by replacing the US-made parts with French-made components.[^cite_note-Reuters-186][^cite_note-187] The Egyptian Air Force is interested in buying the Rafale F4 variant once Dassault prepares it for foreign buyers.[^cite_note-188]

Analysts view the relatively quick series of 84[^cite_note-aw2015-05-13-189] orders from Egypt and Qatar as being influenced by the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) and uncertainty of US involvement in the Middle East.[^cite_note-190]

### Qatar



Qatari Rafale at Bordeaux–Mérignac Airport in 2019

The [Qatar Emiri Air Force](4014890.md) evaluated the Rafale alongside the [Boeing F/A-18E/F Super Hornet](845012.md), the [Boeing F-15E](495724.md), the Eurofighter Typhoon and the [Lockheed Martin F-35 Lightning II](11812.md) to replace its [Dassault Mirage 2000-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_2000#Mirage_2000-5F) fleet. In June 2014, Dassault claimed it was close to signing a contract with Qatar for 72 Rafales.[^cite_note-191] On 30 April 2015, [Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh) [Tamim bin Hamad Al Thani](6146151.md) announced to French President [François Hollande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Hollande) that Qatar would order 24 Rafale with an option to buy 12 more aircraft.[^cite_note-\:0-192] On 4 May, a €6.3 billion (\$7.02 billion) contract for 24 Rafales was finalised; additionally, the contract included the provision of long-range cruise missiles and Meteor missiles as well as the training of 36 Qatari pilots and 100 technicians by the French military and several Qatari intelligence officers; thus, the price can be viewed as €263M for each aircraft.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-194]

On 7 December 2017, the option for 12 more Rafales was exercised for €1.1 billion (or €92M each) while adding an additional option for 36 further fighters.[^cite_note-Le_Monde-195] The first Qatari Rafale was delivered in February 2019.[^cite_note-QatarJanes-196][^cite_note-FlightGlobalQatar-197]

### India

#### Indian Air Force

Main articles: [Indian MRCA competition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_MRCA_competition) and [Rafale deal controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafale_deal_controversy)



IAF Rafale at [Bordeaux–Mérignac Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bordeaux%E2%80%93M%C3%A9rignac_Airport), 6 February 2020

The Rafale was one of the six aircraft competing in the [Indian MRCA competition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_MRCA_competition) for 126 multirole fighters. Originally, the Mirage 2000 had been considered for the competition, but Dassault withdrew it in favour of the Rafale.[^cite_note-198] In February 2011, French Rafales flew demonstrations in India, including air-to-air combat against [Su-30MKIs](3793034.md).[^cite_note-Times_of_India-199] In April 2011, the [Indian Air Force](403355.md) (IAF) shortlisted the Rafale and Eurofighter Typhoon for the US\$10.4 billion contract.[^cite_note-200] On 31 January 2012, the IAF announced the Rafale as the preferred bidder.[^cite_note-Times-201][^cite_note-MMRCA_Rafale-202] It was proposed that 18 Rafales would be supplied to the IAF by 2015 in fly-away condition, while the remaining 108 would be manufactured by [Hindustan Aeronautics Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan_Aeronautics_Limited) (HAL) in India under [transfer of technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technology_transfer) agreements.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] The contract for 126 Rafales, services, and parts may have been worth up to US\$20 billion.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206]

The deal stalled due to disagreements over local production; Dassault refused responsibility for the 108 HAL-manufactured Rafales, holding reservations over HAL's ability to accommodate the complex manufacturing and technology transfers; instead, Dassault said it would have to negotiate two separate production contracts by both companies. The [Indian Defence Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(India\)) instead wanted Dassault to be solely responsible for the sale and delivery of all 126 aircraft.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] In May 2013, *[The Times of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_India)* reported that negotiations were "back on track", with plans for the first 18 Rafales to be delivered in 2017.[^cite_note-209] In March 2014, the two sides reportedly agreed that the first 18 Rafales would be delivered to India in flying condition and that the remaining 108 would be 70 percent built by HAL.[^cite_note-210] By December 2014, India and France reportedly expected to sign a contract by March 2015.[^cite_note-211]

In April 2015, during Prime Minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md)'s visit to Paris, India requested the swift delivery of 36 Rafales in a fly-away condition.[^cite_note-Dassault_India_to_buy_36-212][^cite_note-213] India withdrew the MMRCA tender on 30 July 2015.[^cite_note-214] Then, India and France missed a July target to finalise the 36-aircraft deal. The previously agreed-upon terms in April totaled US\$8 billion for 36 aircraft costing \$200 million each, with an offset requirement of 30 percent of the deal's value to be reinvested in India's defence sector and infrastructure for Rafale operations. India insisted on a 50 percent offset and two bases, which France said would increase costs and require separate infrastructure and two sets of maintenance, training and armament storage facilities.[^cite_note-215] On 23 September 2016, Defence Minister [Manohar Parrikar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manohar_Parrikar) and his French counterpart [Jean-Yves Le Drian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Le_Drian) signed a €7.8 billion contract for 36 fly-away Rafales with an option for 18 more.[^cite_note-Janes_India_finally_signs-216] Initial deliveries were expected by 2019, and all 36 within six years.[^cite_note-217] The deal included spares and weapons such as [Meteor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteor_\(missile\)) missiles.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] Around August 2017, India considered ordering 36 more Rafales amid tensions with China.[^cite_note-220]

The [Indian National Congress](149333.md) raised an issue over Dassault partnering with [Anil Ambani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anil_Ambani)'s [Reliance Defence and Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reliance_Defence_and_Engineering), a private company with no aviation experience, instead of the state owned HAL. Allegedly, Dassault lacked any choice and was compelled to select Reliance Defence as its partner. [Rahul Gandhi](305010.md) alleged that it was favouritism and corruption. Both the French government and Dassault issued a press release stating it was Dassault's decision to choose Reliance Defence.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] Party spokesperson [Manish Tewari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manish_Tewari) asked for the agreement's details to be made public and questioned if there was an escalation of per-aircraft cost from ₹7.15 billion to ₹16 billion.[^cite_note-223] In November 2018, Congress alleged that procurement procedures were bypassed. A Public Interest Litigation (PIL) case was filed in the [Supreme Court](395852.md) for an independent probe into the Rafale procurement. On 14 December 2018, the Apex Court dismissed all petitions, stating it found no irregularities; Reliance Defence reportedly was set to receive just over 3 per cent of the ₹300 billion (equivalent to ₹440 billion or US\$4.5 billion in 2026) of offsets, contrary to the impression that it was to be the biggest beneficiary of the deal.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]



A Rafale landing at [Ambala Air Force Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambala_Air_Force_Station) on its first arrival in India on 29 July 2020.

Ahead of the first Rafale's formal handover on 8 October 2019 (IAF Day), the IAF accepted it at Dassault's Bordeaux facility in an event attended by Defence Minister [Rajnath Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajnath_Singh) and his French counterpart, [Florence Parly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence_Parly); it had tail number "RB-001" in reference to IAF chief-designate [Air Chief Marshal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_chief_marshal_\(India\)) [R. K. S. Bhadauria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._K._S._Bhadauria).[^cite_note-226] The first five Rafales were delivered on 27 July 2020.[^cite_note-227] The last Rafale arrived in April 2022.[^cite_note-228]

In June 2024, an IAF contingent attended the second edition [Red Flag – Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Flag_%E2%80%93_Alaska) 2024 exercise which was conducted from 4 June to 14 June in [Eielson Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eielson_Air_Force_Base), [Alaska](624.md). This contingent consisted of Rafales, one [Il-78MKI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyushin_Il-78) mid-air refueller and a [C-17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_C-17_Globemaster_III) heavy transport aircraft. The exercise focused on Beyond Visual Range combat simulations. Other participants in the exercise included [Republic of Singapore Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Singapore_Air_Force), [Royal Air Force](25679.md), [Royal Netherlands Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Netherlands_Air_Force), [Luftwaffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Air_Force), and the [US Air Force](32090.md). The aggressor unit was [18th Fighter Interceptor Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/18th_Fighter_Interceptor_Squadron). After the conclusion of the exercise, on the way back to India, the contingent made a refuelling stop at [Lajes Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lajes_Field), [Portugal](23033.md). After the stop, the contingent was split into two components, with one visiting Greece and the other visiting Egypt.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] The Rafales also participated in air combat exercises with [Hellenic Air Force](363254.md)'s [F-16](11642.md) and [Egyptian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force)'s Rafales.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232]

During 2024, IAF Rafales at [Hasimara AFS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasimara_Air_Force_Station) under the aegis of [Eastern Air Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Air_Command_\(India\)) had reportedly been trained to shoot air-to-air missiles at targets mimicking Chinese spy balloons at relatively high altitude of 55,000 ft (17,000 m); this occurred after the [2023 Chinese balloon incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Chinese_balloon_incident).[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234]

The IAF's Rafale fleet will receive upgrades through the Indian Navy's Rafale Marine deal. For this, 10 of the 36 IAF Rafales will be upgraded to allow buddy-buddy refuelling enhancing their operational range. Other improvements include ground-based support equipment, software upgrades, advanced weapon systems and sensor suites as well as 40 long-range [drop tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drop_tanks).[^cite_note-\:10-235][^cite_note-\:9-236][^cite_note-\:11-237]

##### 2025 India–Pakistan conflict

Main article: [2025 India–Pakistan conflict](79896622.md)

On the intervening night of 6–7 May 2025, the IAF reportedly deployed Rafales armed with [SCALP missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SCALP_missile) and [AASM Hammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AASM_Hammer) glide bombs during Operation Sindoor to strike nine targets within Pakistan, which India alleged to be terrorist camps.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] According to Pakistani authorities, five Indian aircraft were shot down during the operation, including three Rafales.[^cite_note-241] Concurrently, [CNN](62028.md) reported the loss of one Rafale cited with claims of an unnamed high ranking French intelligence official,[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] while [Reuters](18998750.md) separately reported that Pakistani J-10 fighters were believed to have shot down at least two Indian aircraft, including a Rafale, citing claims from two unnamed U.S. officials.[^cite_note-reuters_250508-244] Pakistan itself implied using [J-10C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J-10C) fighters in the combat, as well as the use of [PL-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PL-15) long-range air-to-air missiles.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248] The [Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post) reported that the debris consistently matched with at least two French-made IAF aircraft — one of them being a Rafale. One verified image showed the wreckage of the vertical stabilizer of a Rafale; the [National Interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Interest) said that, based on this evidence, at least one Rafale was indeed lost.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250] Additionally, [BBC Verify](74154160.md) reported that a Rafale had crashed in Indian territory. The unit authenticated three videos showing the wreckage of a Rafale in a field near Bathinda, Punjab. One video, geo-located by BBC Verify, showed Indian troops collecting debris from the crash site during daylight. Two additional clips from the same location, filmed at night, showed debris in a field and a projectile catching fire in the sky before igniting on the ground.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252]

According to French [deputy](29334549.md) [Marc Chavent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Chavent), several sources - including American analysts and NATO intelligence data - credited the J-10C with destroying a Rafale.[^cite_note-chavent_2025-05-253] On 9 May, the [Pakistan Air Force](377433.md) reiterated its earlier downing claims of five Indian fighters, including Rafales, backed by crash locations, how the interception of combat formations was conducted, Cockpit Voice Recorder (CVR) transcript of the formation leader purportedly expressing concern over losing contact with a fellow pilot, and flight data.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256]

On 31 May 2025, India's [Chief of Defence Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_Defence_Staff_\(India\)) [General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_\(India\)) [Anil Chauhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anil_Chauhan) admitted that India had lost jets, but dismissed Pakistan's claim to have shot down six fighters.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] According to Chauhan, these losses led to corrections in India's tactics in the following strikes of 7, 8 and 10 May.[^cite_note-Reuters_tactics-259][^cite_note-TheHindu_losses-260]

Following India's airstrikes against Pakistan, anonymous French officials cited by the *[Associated Press](18935732.md)* alleged that China had engaged in a coordinated effort to undermine international sales of Rafales. French military officials said that they had not found sufficient evidence to directly link online posts criticizing the performance of Rafales to the Chinese government; however, French intelligence services reported that Chinese embassy defence attachés conveyed the same narratives during meetings with security officials from other countries, including claims that IAF Rafales had not performed well and promoted Chinese-made weaponry.
